What makes the three work together is that each closes a different door — who can buy the technology, what can be built, and what a developer treats as normal — and an authoritarian government, or a company willing to serve one, has to get through all three rather than just whichever one happens to apply in its jurisdiction.
Chapter 6: Collaborative Research and Development
Chapter 5 asked what a design can do to resist authoritarian capture — technical safeguards, policy levers that raise the cost of misuse. None of that gets built by one person working alone. This chapter is about the people and institutions that have to coordinate for it to happen at all: researchers across disciplines who do not start out sharing a vocabulary, a public whose consent the work depends on but rarely has, research communities and governments that agree on a handful of shared standards and disagree on almost everything else, and the different regional rules any of this has to operate inside.

Section 6.1 takes up interdisciplinary collaboration — the practical mechanics of getting a neuroscientist, an ethicist, and a machine-learning researcher to work together rather than past each other. Section 6.2 turns to the public: what genuine stakeholder engagement looks like, as against the appearance of it. Section 6.3 covers cooperation between researchers and institutions across borders, including the technical standards the engineering community has built on its own, distinct from the treaty-and-declaration landscape chapter 7 covers. Section 6.4 closes with what happens where those norms meet a specific jurisdiction's law, which is rarely the same law twice.
6.1. Interdisciplinary Collaboration and Knowledge Exchange
Building benevolent superintelligent AI takes more than one field. Machine learning sets what a system can do; cognitive science and moral psychology describe what actually tracking human welfare would require of it; ethics asks whether the result is good. None of these fields gets the system right by itself, and how they combine matters as much as what each contributes on its own.

Funding structured around that convergence — a grant that requires a machine-learning lab and an ethics or psychology department to work the same problem rather than adjacent ones — does something a single discipline's normal grant cycle does not: it puts people who would not otherwise share a budget, a dataset, or a deadline into the same project. Section 6.1.2 covers what that funding has looked like in practice.

Workshops and conferences that gather a broad spectrum of experts do similar work on a shorter cycle, giving ideas, strategies, and failure modes a room to be argued over rather than published past each other in separate journals. Stanford's One Hundred Year Study on Artificial Intelligence, launched in 2014 with a gift from computer scientist Eric Horvitz and Mary Horvitz, is a working example: rather than a single report, it convenes a new standing panel roughly every five years to assess where AI's advance has actually changed a specific set of human concerns, from healthcare to employment [[cite:C0451]].

Open access to research resources, methodologies, and findings works on a different axis: instead of putting people in the same room, it removes the reason not to build on someone else's work. The Partnership on AI, covered in more detail in section 7.5, is built on that premise at industry scale — that best practice develops faster in the open than behind a dozen labs' separate closed doors.

Sections 6.1.1 through 6.1.3 take up three of these mechanisms in turn: cross-disciplinary communication and cooperation, joint research programs, and shared platforms and tools.
6.1.1. Cross-disciplinary Communication and Cooperation
Cross-disciplinary communication matters only where it actually happens: a conference, a lab, a classroom, a shared codebase. Four venues do most of that work.

The AAAI/ACM Conference on AI, Ethics, and Society, first held in 2018, brings researchers, policymakers, and technologists together specifically on AI's ethical and societal questions, rather than treating them as a side track of a general machine-learning venue [[cite:C0452]]. NeurIPS, primarily a machine-learning venue itself, has hosted a recurring Workshop on Machine Learning Safety since 2022, run in partnership with the Center for AI Safety, pulling alignment-focused machine-learning researchers into the same room as philosophers and psychologists working the same problem from a different direction [[cite:C0453]].

Joint research centers do similar work on a longer timescale. The Center for Human-Compatible AI at UC Berkeley, founded in 2016 under computer scientist Stuart Russell, pairs AI researchers with cognitive scientists and philosophers at partner universities — an arrangement no single department could staff on its own [[cite:C0454]].

Academic programs can build the next generation of researchers to already think this way, rather than retrofitting the habit later. Embedded EthiCS, developed at Harvard beginning in 2017 by computer scientist Barbara Grosz and philosopher Alison Simmons, pairs philosophy graduate students with computer-science faculty to write ethics modules directly into standard CS courses, instead of routing ethics through a single stand-alone class students can skip [[cite:C0455]].

Industry-academia partnerships are a fourth channel. The Partnership on AI, discussed in section 7.5, is the clearest standing example: a consortium built to fund and convene cross-disciplinary AI research across companies, universities, and civil-society groups, rather than leave it to any one member to run alone. Shared digital platforms and tools that let researchers collaborate remotely, rather than only at a scheduled event, are a distinct enough mechanism to warrant their own account in section 6.1.3.
6.1.2. Establishing Joint Research Initiatives and Programs
Joint research initiatives turn interdisciplinary collaboration from an event into an institution — a standing lab, a competition, a grant program that outlasts whoever wrote the first proposal.

Academic institutes do this by giving interdisciplinary AI-ethics work a permanent home inside a university rather than a one-off grant. Stanford's Institute for Human-Centered AI, launched in 2019 and co-directed by computer scientist Fei-Fei Li and philosopher John Etchemendy, is one example [[cite:C0456]]. The Ethics and Governance of Artificial Intelligence Initiative, launched in 2017 by MIT's Media Lab and Harvard's Berkman Klein Center together, is another — built explicitly as a joint project between the two institutions rather than housed in just one [[cite:C0457]].

Competitions do similar work by giving an interdisciplinary team a shared, real-world problem to solve together rather than a shared committee to sit on. Section 4.5.3 covers the IBM Watson AI XPRIZE as the clearest example of that structure working as designed.

Funding programs are the more direct lever, and the more specific ones matter more than the general ones. The National Science Foundation's Fairness in Artificial Intelligence program, run jointly with Amazon since 2019, funds research specifically at the intersection of computer science and the social-science work of defining what "fair" means for a given system [[cite:C0458]]. On a larger scale, the European Commission's SHERPA project, funded under Horizon 2020 from 2018 to 2022, put eleven partner organizations across six countries — spanning academia, industry, civil society, and standards bodies — to work on the ethical and human-rights dimensions of AI and big-data systems together, rather than in separate national silos [[cite:C0459]].

Joint programs built explicitly around cross-border, government-to-government coordination are a related but distinct mechanism, covered in section 6.3.1. What belongs here is the funding and institutional structure that makes interdisciplinary work possible in the first place, wherever it happens.
6.1.3. Developing Shared Platforms and Tools for an AI Research Ecosystem
Funding and forums get researchers talking to each other. Shared infrastructure lets them actually work on the same material — the same dataset, the same codebase, the same evaluation suite — which is a different and harder problem.

Some of this already exists. AI Ethics Lab, founded in 2017 by philosopher Cansu Canca, is a standing practice hub where computer scientists, legal scholars, and philosophers work through specific AI-ethics problems together, rather than publishing separate takes on the same question [[cite:C0460]]. The AI Alignment Forum, built in 2018 by the team behind LessWrong to replace a narrower forum the Machine Intelligence Research Institute had maintained, does the same work for technical alignment research specifically, giving researchers a shared place to post work in progress rather than wait for a conference cycle [[cite:C0461]]. Section 4.5.3 covers a third piece of this same infrastructure: the Hugging Face Transformers library, and what open-source tooling does for reproducibility across labs.

What is missing is the equivalent for evaluation. A shared benchmark that measured ethical reasoning, empathetic response, and altruistic behavior the way existing benchmarks measure raw task performance would let separate labs' claims about a system's ethical behavior actually be compared, rather than taken on the strength of whichever paper described them best.

Section 6.1.2 covers what a competition built around a shared real-world problem, rather than a leaderboard metric, can do toward that same goal.
6.2. Stakeholder Involvement and Public Engagement

Democratizing AI development means building the people affected by a system into how it gets built, not just consulting them once it already exists. Regulators set the legal boundaries around data, privacy, and accountability. Developers and researchers understand a system's actual capabilities and failure modes better than anyone outside the lab. End users find the places a design breaks down in practice that no design team anticipated. Communities already carrying the costs of the digital divide have the clearest view of who a system leaves out. Civil-society organizations raise the political questions a technologist is prone to missing. Leave any one of these groups out, and a decision that affects all of them gets made by whoever is left in the room.

One structural way to build that involvement is the citizen jury: a panel of randomly selected, non-expert citizens deliberates on a specific question and produces recommendations that feed into policy. Section 4.4.6 covers Taiwan's vTaiwan process, the clearest working example of that approach applied specifically to AI.

Sections 6.2.1 through 6.2.3 take up three pieces of what this looks like in practice: bringing diverse stakeholders directly into development work, building the structures that let the public deliberate on AI governance, and building the literacy that makes public input worth having in the first place.
6.2.1. Engaging Diverse Stakeholders in AI Development

An AI system designed by one kind of person, inside one kind of institution, tends to reproduce that person's blind spots at scale. Bringing in people with different expertise, backgrounds, and stakes in the outcome is what catches a problem before deployment rather than after.

<<list>>
1. Cross-sector research collaboration. Funding joint work across industry, academia, and civil society surfaces problems no single sector would catch alone. The University of Cambridge's Leverhulme Centre for the Future of Intelligence, launched in 2016 with a £10 million grant from the Leverhulme Trust, brings together philosophers, machine-learning researchers, and scholars from fields including history, engineering, and media studies to study AI's effects on society [[cite:C0471]]. Section 7.5 covers the Partnership on AI, the industry consortium built for cross-sector coordination at a larger scale.

2. Inclusive design workshops. Putting ethicists, social scientists, and members of an affected community in the same room as the engineers building a system catches a problem while it is still a prototype. Data & Society, a research institute studying the social implications of data-centric technology, runs workshops built for exactly this, including its AI.Assembly Workshop, which brings together artists, academics, technologists, and humanists of color to work through how AI systems affecting their communities get designed and governed [[cite:C0472]].

3. Ethics advisory boards. A review board only works if it can say no to something. IBM created its AI Ethics Board after publishing three company-wide AI principles in 2018; the board, co-chaired since 2019, is a cross-company body that reviews how IBM builds and uses AI and rules on the hard cases against those principles [[cite:C0473]].

4. Community-driven development. A system built with the people who will use it, rather than for them, is more likely to fit the problem they actually have. That means treating local knowledge as a design input, not a focus-group afterthought, especially where a system is meant to serve a community outside the one that built it.
<</list>>

Section 6.2.2 takes up the fifth piece directly: bringing the public itself, not just the organizations that claim to represent it, into AI governance.
6.2.2. Public Deliberation and Participatory AI Governance

Section 6.2.1 covers the standing partnerships already built between AI developers, government, and civil society, and section 7.5 covers what those partnerships look like at the international level. What is specific to governance, rather than development, is the machinery built to bring public input into a decision at a single point in time rather than through an ongoing partnership.

<<list>>
1. Citizen assemblies. A panel of citizens selected through random, stratified sampling deliberates on a question over an extended period and produces recommendations grounded in that deliberation rather than a snap poll. Ireland's Citizens' Assembly, first convened in 2016, has used exactly this format to shape the country's recommendations on abortion law and on its response to climate change — evidence the model works on questions with the same mix of technical complexity and moral stakes AI governance has, even though no assembly has yet been convened on AI itself [[cite:C0474]].

2. Online platforms for public input. A standing online consultation reaches people a physical assembly cannot, at the cost of the depth an extended, facilitated deliberation provides. The European Commission's 2020 public consultation on its AI White Paper drew more than 1,200 responses from citizens, companies, and civil-society groups across the EU, helping shape the legislative proposal that became the EU AI Act [[cite:C0475]].

3. AI ethics committees. An ethics board answerable only to the company that created it is only as independent as that company is willing to let it be. DeepMind Health set up an outside panel in 2016 to review its work with the UK's National Health Service and publish annual reports on its findings; Google disbanded the panel in 2019, after members had raised concerns about how much access to information, and how much real independence from the company, they actually had [[cite:C0476]]. The panel's short life is its own governance lesson: a review body is worth only as much as the independence it can actually enforce.
<</list>>

Section 6.2.3 covers the literacy work that has to happen before any of these mechanisms produces input worth having — a citizen assembly or an online consultation is only as good as the public's ability to understand what it is being asked.
6.2.3. AI Literacy Outside the Classroom

Section 7.1.5 covers how AI literacy gets built into schools, universities, and dedicated public courses, Finland's Elements of AI among them. What is different here is bringing AI into the places people already are — a museum visit, a policy debate, a town hall — rather than a course somebody has to seek out.

<<list>>
1. Public engagement on specific policy questions. The Ada Lovelace Institute has run public engagement on facial recognition specifically, including a Citizens' Biometrics Council that brought 50 members of the UK public together to deliberate on how biometric technology should be regulated, and published the first national survey of UK public opinion on the technology [[cite:C0477]]. Section 6.2.2 covers the citizen-assembly format this borrows from.

2. Museum and science-center exhibitions. The MIT Museum's permanent "Robots and Beyond" exhibition, opened in 2022, puts 50 years of MIT's AI and robotics research in front of a visitor who will never take a computer science course, tracing the field from early robots like Kismet and Cog through work happening at the Computer Science and Artificial Intelligence Laboratory today [[cite:C0478]].

3. Direct public outreach. Researchers and practitioners visiting schools, community centers, and local events to answer questions and explain what an AI system actually does reaches people neither a curriculum nor a museum exhibit does on its own.
<</list>>
6.3. International Cooperation and Norm Development
No single country can bind AI development to altruistic and anti-authoritarian values on its own. Section 6.3.1 covers how research institutions build the working relationships cross-border AI research depends on. Section 6.3.2 turns to standards built by the technical community itself, which is a different layer from the treaty-and-declaration landscape chapter 7 covers in depth. Section 6.3.3 closes with what international AI collaboration can do for problems that are not primarily about AI ethics at all — climate, health, poverty — and what it cannot.
6.3.1. How Research Institutions Cooperate Across Borders

Altruistic, anti-authoritarian AI cannot be built inside one country's research institutions alone; training data, safety techniques, and researchers themselves cross borders more easily than most other engineering work does. What that cooperation actually looks like is narrower than a treaty and less formal than a certification body: shared tools, shared training runs, and shared publication norms — the layer underneath both the technical standards section 6.3.2 covers and the government-to-government structures section 7.5 covers.

The clearest example is also one of the oldest. OpenAI released Gym, an open-source toolkit for building and comparing reinforcement-learning algorithms, in 2016 [[cite:C0491]]. Any lab working on reinforcement learning, in any country, could build against the same benchmark environments instead of writing its own, and results from competing labs became directly comparable because their authors had trained against the same suite. That is not evidence of a formal partnership between OpenAI and DeepMind: the two organizations are commercial competitors for the same funding, researchers, and talent, and no partnership between them is documented. It is evidence of something a treaty cannot produce on its own — shared infrastructure any lab can build on regardless of what its home government has signed.

A more recent example runs the same logic at a larger scale. The BigScience workshop, coordinated by Hugging Face over 2021 and 2022, brought together more than a thousand researchers from over seventy countries to build BLOOM, a 176-billion-parameter multilingual language model released under a license written specifically to restrict harmful use, its training data and code made public rather than proprietary to any single company or government [[cite:C0492]]. Nothing compelled any of those researchers to take part, and nothing compels the next comparable effort to happen at all: the model exists because enough institutions decided cooperating on this specific project was worth more than competing on it, not because any structure required it.

A standing, better-funded version of the same idea already exists at two levels: the Global Partnership on AI at the government level, and the Partnership on AI at the industry-and-academic level. Section 7.5 covers both in the context of the wider treaty-and-declaration landscape; nothing in this section's own research turns up a gap those two bodies leave unfilled that a new standing consortium would close. What research institutions actually get from international cooperation looks more like the Gym and BLOOM examples above: shared tools and shared data any lab can build on, not another committee. Closing the resource gap that keeps most of the world's research institutions from participating in either is a harder problem than a partnership can solve by itself; section 7.5's account of the digital divide covers what has and has not worked.
6.3.2. Standards Built by the Technical Community, Not by Governments
Section 7.5 covers the treaty-and-declaration landscape states negotiate directly: a binding Council of Europe convention, the OECD's recommendation, UNESCO's, the two industry-and-government partnerships, the safety-institute network built after the Bletchley Declaration. A different, older layer of standard-setting sits underneath all of it, built by engineers rather than diplomats, and it deserves its own account rather than a line inside that survey.

The IEEE's Ethically Aligned Design, first published in 2019, set out eight general principles for autonomous and intelligent systems and, more consequentially, a follow-on series of engineering standards — the IEEE 7000 series — that translate those principles into something a development team can actually test against: value-based requirements engineering (IEEE 7000, 2021) and a well-being impact assessment (IEEE 7010), among a growing list addressing bias, transparency, and algorithmic provenance [[cite:C0448]]. The document informed the OECD's own AI Principles the same year, among other governmental efforts — engineers wrote a vocabulary for what an ethical AI system does before most governments had written a rule about it [[cite:C0449]].

ISO/IEC 42001, published in December 2023, took that layer further: the first management-system standard for AI, structured like the standards a company already uses for information security or quality control, specifying what a functioning AI governance program inside an organization has to include — risk assessment, an impact assessment for each system, oversight of third-party suppliers — and auditable by an accredited certification body the way those other standards are [[cite:C0450]].

What a technical standard does that a treaty cannot is bind through the market rather than through a state's consent. A government uninterested in constraining AI has no reason to ratify a human-rights convention; a company that wants to sell into a market, or to a customer, that requires ISO 42001 certification has a reason regardless of what its home government thinks of the standard's content. That advantage has real limits — a company operating entirely inside a market that never asks for the certification has no reason to seek it either, and certification measures whether a management system exists, not whether the system it describes is followed with any rigor day to day. It is a different lever from the ones section 7.5 covers, not a substitute for them, and it reaches an authoritarian government's own AI systems, built and used domestically with no export or customer relationship at stake, not at all.
6.3.3. What International AI Collaboration Can and Cannot Fix

AI collaboration reaches furthest where the underlying problem is inherently cross-border and no single country's data is sufficient on its own. Climate is the clearest case: weather and climate systems do not respect national boundaries, and a model trained only on one region's data predicts poorly everywhere else. The Climate, Land, Energy and Water Systems (CLEWS) framework — coordinated by the UN Department of Economic and Social Affairs, the International Atomic Energy Agency, and the UN Development Programme, with a capacity-building program that has reached participants in more than twenty countries since 2015 — is real and genuinely cross-border, and worth describing precisely: it is an integrated systems-optimization framework, and the research literature around it treats machine learning as a promising addition still being built in, not a feature the framework already has [[cite:C0493]]. Where AI collaboration does the specific climate work this section is about, it currently looks more like national weather services training and benchmarking forecasting models against shared data than like a single named research consortium.

Disease surveillance is a similar case for the same underlying reason: a pathogen crossing from an animal population into a human one, or from one country into the next, does not stay inside the jurisdiction that first detects it. The Global Virome Project, formally proposed in a 2018 paper in Science as a successor to USAID's PREDICT program, set out to catalog the viral families with the highest zoonotic spillover potential across dozens of countries before the next pandemic rather than after it [[cite:C0494]]. The AI in this picture sits downstream of the cataloging, not in place of it: the sequence data a project like this generates is what spillover-risk prediction models get trained on, which makes the international data-sharing arrangement itself, not any single algorithm, the part actually worth calling collaboration.

Poverty and economic development are where this kind of cross-border AI collaboration is least developed, not most. The general claims are easy to make: AI-driven data analysis can reveal patterns of inequality that guide where aid and investment go, and can make supply chains, agricultural forecasting, and access to financial services work better in places that currently have none of it. A specific, verifiable international partnership doing sustained work at that scale, the way CLEWS or the Global Virome Project does for climate and health, is harder to find than the previous two paragraphs might suggest. What exists at the institutional level is the digital-divide problem section 7.5 covers in detail: uneven data, uneven compute, uneven regulatory capacity, and a recurring set of proposed remedies, capacity building and technology transfer among them, that has not yet closed the gap.

Conflict prevention is a fourth domain where the underlying data problem — dispersed, multilingual, and produced by people with no interest in cooperating with researchers — makes international collaboration closer to a precondition than an option. PeaceTech Lab, an independent nonprofit that grew out of a US Institute of Peace project launched in 2008 and became independent in 2014, is a working example: its hate-speech lexicon project has combined machine learning with local-language workshops and interviews to build region-specific glossaries of inflammatory language in at least eleven countries, including Kenya, Nigeria, Iraq, and Yemen — ground-level data collaboration that international peacebuilding tools depend on and rarely get [[cite:C0495]].

None of these four cases is evidence that AI collaboration works the way the treaty layer aspires to. Each ran on the researchers and institutions involved choosing to participate, project by project, with no enforcement mechanism binding the next one to happen and no guarantee that a government hostile to the work could not simply keep its own researchers out of it. Section 5.4.1 catalogs what a government willing to point AI at its own population does with tools built for exactly these humanitarian purposes elsewhere; a government can fund pandemic-surveillance research of the kind described above and, separately, build the biometric-surveillance systems that section documents, and nothing about participating in one constrains the other.
6.4. Regional Regulatory Frameworks for AI Development
No region regulates AI the same way. Section 6.4.1 compares what a handful of them have actually done, and how little of it harmonizes across a border. Section 6.4.2 turns to what adapting an AI system to a specific culture requires, and what it does not license.
6.4.1. Comparing and Harmonizing Regional AI Rules
Section 7.5 compares how the European Union, the United States, the United Kingdom, and China actually regulate AI within their own borders: a comprehensive risk-tiered statute, a sectoral patchwork built on agencies that predate AI, principles routed through existing regulators, and a licensing regime built to police a communications channel rather than a product. Those four already diverge sharply. The regions that comparison leaves out diverge again, in different directions, and what actually crosses a border between any of them turns out to be narrower than a shared statute.

Canada regulates its own federal government's use of AI through a binding rule, the 2019 Directive on Automated Decision-Making, which requires an algorithmic impact assessment, scaled to the decision's stakes, before a federal system can make or assist a decision that affects a person [[cite:C0501]]. A broader statute meant to extend comparable obligations to private companies, the Artificial Intelligence and Data Act, spent three years moving through Parliament and then died with it: when Parliament was prorogued in January 2025, the underlying bill, C-27, died on the order paper, and had not been reintroduced as of mid-2026. A framework that depends on one government's survival is a fragile thing for a trading partner to harmonize with.

Singapore took the opposite structural bet: no statute, a voluntary Model AI Governance Framework that its privacy regulator and infocomm authority first published in 2019 and revised in 2020, giving companies detailed but non-binding guidance on internal AI governance [[cite:C0502]]. Since 2022, a companion toolkit called AI Verify has let a company test its own system against the framework's principles and produce a report a regulator, a customer, or the public can inspect. It is the UK's wager taken further: not just guidance routed through an existing regulator, but guidance backed by no regulator's legal mandate at all, only the market pressure of a public test result.

Taiwan took a third path, and only recently. Its Legislative Yuan passed an AI Basic Act in December 2025, promulgated the following month as the country's first dedicated AI statute: twenty articles built around seven principles, among them transparency and explainability, fairness and non-discrimination, and accountability, administered by the National Science and Technology Council [[cite:C0503]]. The principles echo the UK's five almost point for point. What differs is the instrument: Taiwan wrote them into binding statute rather than administrative guidance, landing somewhere between the EU's comprehensive-statute model and the UK's lighter one.

One place harmonization is actually happening, rather than being proposed, is narrower than any of this: cross-border data flows. In March 2022, the US Department of Commerce and Singapore's trade and infocomm authorities agreed to align their AI governance frameworks and expand Singapore's participation in APEC's Cross-Border Privacy Rules System, a certification that lets a company demonstrate it meets one common privacy standard and have every participating economy recognize that single certification rather than auditing the company separately in each [[cite:C0504]]. That is what cross-border harmonization actually looks like in practice: not a shared global framework, but a small set of governments willing to certify to the same standard and recognize each other's certifications.

None of this converges toward a single global standard, and nothing here suggests it will. What actually crosses a border is a certification a handful of regulators agree to recognize, a technical standard a company adopts because a customer requires it, or a treaty obligation written at the level of human rights rather than a specific rule — section 6.3.2 covers the first two mechanisms, and section 7.5 covers the third. Harmonizing regional AI rules is less a matter of writing one shared framework than of finding, case by case, a mechanism a government working from a different premise still has reason to follow.
6.4.2. Adapting Regulatory Frameworks and Ethical Considerations to Local Contexts
Adapting AI regulation and ethical practice to local context means responding to the actual needs, preferences, and values of a given community, not making preposterous reductions such as "Chinese people actually like having a police state because it is their culture." A system built to work well has to be sensitive to real cultural, social, and moral differences: an AI system developed in a Western country may treat individual autonomy as the default good, one developed in an East Asian country may treat group harmony as the default instead, and both assumptions have something to recommend them — autonomy supports creativity and innovation, harmony supports cooperation and stability. Recognizing that difference is not license to endorse whatever a government built on top of it does to its own population; it is a design constraint, not a political alibi.

Designing an AI system that holds up across differences like these takes four things.

<<list>>
1. Diverse perspectives built in from the start. Developers and ethicists working with stakeholders outside their own cultural, social, and moral background catch blind spots the design process would otherwise miss. Indigenous communities helping shape an AI system for wildlife conservation or environmental monitoring are the ones who actually hold the knowledge those systems depend on.
2. Local value alignment without abandoning the baseline. A system does not need to import every local norm uncritically, but it needs room to adjust: an end-of-life care system has to weigh region- and family-specific religious and cultural beliefs case by case, not apply one default across every patient, if it is going to earn the trust of the people it serves.
3. Sustained cross-cultural dialogue, not a one-time consultation. Conferences and working sessions that keep running past a launch date surface ethical blind spots that a single round of feedback misses.
4. A shared, maintained record of what other regions have already tried. Case studies and guidelines from outside one's own country do more good compiled somewhere a developer can actually find them than scattered across whatever venue first produced them.
<</list>>

Inclusion matters for a related reason: an AI system that ignores a society's economic and cultural diversity risks widening the inequalities it could instead help close. A healthcare AI system built around the access, resources, and assumptions of a well-funded hospital underperforms everywhere else. An education AI system built around one learning style, or one set of accessibility assumptions, serves only the learners who already fit it.

Collaboration with the people who actually live in the context a system is meant to serve is what makes the difference in practice, and one concrete example of that at scale is the AI4D program. It launched in 2020 as AI4D Africa, a partnership between Canada's International Development Research Centre and Sweden's international development agency, and expanded in 2024 into a partnership with the UK's Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Office worth more than 100 million Canadian dollars, extending the same model into South and Southeast Asia and the Pacific [[cite:C0505]]. The premise holds at any scale: local researchers, policymakers, and practitioners building the system, rather than receiving one built elsewhere.

<<list>>
1. Partner with local organizations and community groups on AI projects that address problems the community has actually identified.
2. Put local policymakers, researchers, and community members inside the development process itself, not just as reviewers of a finished design.
3. Route AI education and training toward communities that have been underrepresented in the field, rather than assuming the existing talent pool will diversify on its own.
4. Build and share open-source AI tools that local developers can adapt to their own context, rather than exporting a finished system that assumes someone else's.
<</list>>
Chapter 7: Policy Recommendations for AI Ethics, Safety, and Anti-Authoritarianism
Chapter 5 named what can go wrong with an AI system technically — bias, brittleness, misaligned goals, deliberate misuse — and what a design can do about each; chapter 6 covered the collaborative infrastructure that lets researchers, the public, and other governments coordinate on getting it right. This chapter asks who makes a design like that the norm rather than the exception: what economic incentives shape whether it gets built at all, what policy frameworks and industry practice actually enforce, what privacy and security mean for a system trained on people's data, what accountability looks like once a system might be a subject rather than only a tool, and how all of this plays out across jurisdictions that agree on none of it uniformly. None of the technical answers hold on their own, and neither does coordination without enforcement. A well-designed system built inside institutions with no reason to demand one is a design nobody has to use.
7.1. The Political Economy of AI and Power
Concentrated power is close to the default outcome of AI development, not a side effect of it, because the same inputs that make an AI system capable — capital, compute, data, talent — are exactly the inputs that concentrate wherever nothing pushes back. Section 2.1.4 names resistance to market power among AI alignment's anti-authoritarian design goals; this section works out what that resistance takes as economic and political fact, not design principle.

The argument runs in three stages. What economic incentives actually reward shapes what gets built long before any ethics review sees it (7.1.1), and that reward structure concentrates power in a small number of firms with the resources to compete for it at all (7.1.2). Automation then reshapes who works and who gets paid what (7.1.3), which is a problem policy can act on directly rather than only describe (7.1.4). None of the rest is possible to act on without two things this section closes on: a public and a policymaking class that understand AI well enough to govern it (7.1.5), and researchers and developers trained across the disciplines a single-discipline education leaves out (7.1.6).
7.1.1. Economic Incentives in AI Development
Financial incentive does most of the real work in determining what AI development actually produces. Google, Meta, and Amazon commit enormous resources to AI research aimed at operational efficiency, deeper user engagement, and market dominance, and that spending drives genuine technical progress — successive leaps in natural-language processing capability among them [[cite:C0379]]. What that spending does not automatically produce is a system whose outputs serve the people using it rather than the shareholders funding it, and the gap between the two is what this section is about.

A recurring case is the profit-driven AI system that optimizes for engagement at the expense of the people engaged. Recommendation algorithms on social media platforms have been implicated in the spread of misinformation and the hardening of political polarization, because a system tuned to maximize time-on-platform has no built-in reason to prefer accurate content over inflammatory content — the two are not the same objective, and engagement optimization only ever targets the first one [[cite:C0380]].

The same economic logic that rewards engagement over accuracy also rewards scale over access: the firms with the deepest resources for AI research pull further ahead of smaller competitors, independent researchers, and everyone else who lacks comparable capital. Section 7.1.2 traces where that gap leads at the level of the industry as a whole; the point here is only that the incentive starts upstream, in what gets funded in the first place, not downstream in who ends up dominant.

The same incentive structure can run the other way. Public funding for AI research that prioritizes democratic values, inclusivity, and human rights, and government incentives for cross-sector collaboration that keeps AI technology openly accessible, both push development back toward the public interest rather than away from it. Grants and commissions aimed at non-profits building AI tools that broaden the range of perspectives users encounter, rather than narrowing it toward whatever holds attention longest, do the same work from a different angle.

None of this changes the basic shape of the problem: economic incentive, left alone, optimizes for what is profitable rather than what is good for the people on the receiving end of the system, and closing that gap takes deliberate policy rather than the assumption that it will close on its own.
7.1.2. AI and the Concentration of Power in Tech
A small group of firms — Google, Apple, Facebook (now Meta), Amazon, and Microsoft, grouped under the acronym GAFAM — holds a share of AI development disproportionate to their number, and that concentration carries economic and ethical stakes independent of what any single one of them does with the position it occupies. Chapter 4's account of the macrosystem and exosystem pressures around AI development (4.4.3, 4.4.4) already showed why no individual development team escapes this pull; here I trace where the pull leads at the level of the industry as a whole.

Vast financial resources let firms of this size fund extensive research and development, draw the top talent in the field, and command sprawling data networks — the raw material effective machine learning depends on. That combination widens the gap between these firms and smaller companies or new entrants, who cannot match the data or the resources and therefore cannot compete on equal terms.

Acquisition sharpens the same dynamic. Facebook's acquisitions of Instagram and WhatsApp — the company now operates as Meta — did not just remove two competitors; it handed one firm access to comprehensive behavioral data from billions of users worldwide [[cite:C0381]]. Consolidation of that kind extends a firm's control over how AI gets used in social media, advertising, and e-commerce, and it is hard to see from inside a market that concentrated how the trajectory could look like anything other than a foregone conclusion.

Power concentrated this tightly is also power positioned for exploitation, and the risk sharpens considerably when the technology in question reaches an authoritarian government. The relationship between the Chinese state and firms like Alibaba and Tencent is the clearest live example: state access to those firms' machine learning capability has been documented as a channel for surveillance and censorship at scale, which is a serious risk to free expression, individual privacy, and democratic accountability wherever the same model gets exported [[cite:C0382]].

Concentration also has a quieter cost: it blocks competition and slows innovation. A firm that controls the essential datasets and infrastructure a competitive AI system needs functions as a gatekeeper whether or not it intends to, and startups attempting a genuinely different approach are disadvantaged before they start. Fewer independent attempts at AI development means fewer chances at the kind of breakthrough that concentrated incumbents have no incentive to pursue themselves — a real cost to ethical AI development generally, not only to the firms locked out of the market.

Countermeasures exist and are not exotic: antitrust enforcement aimed specifically at AI infrastructure and data control, mandated data-sharing for the datasets that function as chokepoints, transparency requirements that make concentration visible rather than merely inferred, and funding for AI research outside the dominant firms. Section 5.4.3 develops three related mechanisms — export controls, prohibited-practices regulation, and industry design standards — in more empirical detail for the specific case of authoritarian misuse; the concern here is broader and prior to that one: concentration itself, independent of what any single firm chooses to do with the position it concentrates.
7.1.3. AI's Impact on Labor Markets and Income Distribution
As AI systems progressively automate tasks once performed by people, demand for advanced technical skills — AI development, data analysis — grows alongside a shrinking need for the job types most exposed to automation. Left unmanaged, that combination widens income inequality and strains the social fabric across whichever sector it hits hardest.

Manufacturing shows what this looks like at scale. An estimated 5.7 million factory jobs disappeared in the United States between 2000 and 2010, and while economists still dispute how much of that loss traces to automation rather than trade with China, automation and changed production methods account for a substantial share of it [[cite:C0383]]. Retail is following the same path: Amazon opened its first cashier-less Go store to the public in 2018, and stores built on that model displace the specific human roles — cashier, floor sales — that retail automation targets first [[cite:C0384]].

AI development also creates work it did not destroy — research and development, data analysis, AI ethics, human-AI interaction design — but these roles typically demand training that workers displaced from low-skill positions do not have and cannot quickly acquire. The result is a widening split between high-skill and low-skill labor markets, with the low-skill side absorbing most of the displacement and most of the resulting instability.

Meeting that split takes something more direct than encouraging retraining and hoping employers fund it. Employers will only invest in continuous education for their workers at the scale this requires once they cannot afford not to, and the way to force that scenario is a federal job guarantee: government as employer of last resort, hiring anyone who applies, where they live, at a living wage, with paid on-the-job training built into the position rather than offered instead of one. Section 7.1.4 develops job-guarantee programs and the rest of the policy toolkit for workforce disruption in full; the point to establish here is narrower — that a floor under wages and training is what makes the roof possible, not a byproduct of it.

Interdisciplinary research and training funding matters alongside the job guarantee, for a more specific reason: bridging the divide between technical AI development and the fields that study its consequences produces people who can do both, which a labor market retraining displaced factory workers into six-month coding bootcamps mostly does not. I am skeptical that night classes serve this population as well as paid time during the working day would; the point of a job guarantee is to make the education part of the job, not something squeezed around it.
7.1.4. Addressing Workforce Disruption
AI systems capable of automating entire job categories, not just discrete tasks within them, disrupt work at a scale most labor-market institutions were never built to absorb. Section 7.1.3 traces where that disruption is already showing up, in manufacturing and retail specifically; what follows here is the fuller policy response, in seven parts.

<<list>>
1. Retraining and upskilling programs: governments funding retraining through public university systems, rather than leaving it to individual employers, gives workers a path to the skills an AI-driven economy actually demands. Singapore's SkillsFuture initiative, launched in 2015, gives citizens ongoing access to skill-development credits and courses aimed at exactly this kind of competitiveness [[cite:C0385]].

2. Job guarantee programs: a public-sector job at a living wage, available to anyone displaced by automation, does two things retraining alone does not — it gives workers income while they recalibrate, and it directs the labor toward work society already needs: child care, elder care, education, infrastructure, renewable energy, community projects. Section 7.1.3 makes the case for why this has to be the floor beneath the rest of this list rather than one item among several.

3. Social safety nets: unemployment benefits, healthcare, and affordable housing broadened enough to protect people who cannot or choose not to work through a period of displacement. Finland's 2017–2018 basic income experiment paid 2,000 unemployed people a monthly, unconditional stipend and found modestly higher life satisfaction and lower psychological strain among recipients, without a significant negative effect on employment [[cite:C0386]]. I would still put a job guarantee before a basic income on moral grounds: people ought to have the chance to contribute before anyone decides they are unemployable.

4. AI-driven job creation: AI advances in healthcare, for instance, raise demand for the clinicians and technicians who can put diagnostic and personalized-medicine tools to use, rather than only displacing the staff those tools replace.

5. Lifelong learning programs: continuous skill development accessible across career stages and income levels, not concentrated among workers whose employers already invest in them. The European Union's Erasmus+ program, though not AI-specific, is a working example of publicly funded lifelong learning at scale, giving participants cross-border learning experience relevant to a labor market that keeps changing shape [[cite:C0387]].

6. AI-human collaboration: designing work so that AI systems augment human judgment and creativity rather than substitute for them can generate roles that need both — a combination automation alone does not produce and full replacement forecloses.

7. Support for small and medium-sized enterprises: giving smaller firms access to AI resources and expertise lets them compete with the dominant players section 7.1.2 describes, which keeps the AI ecosystem itself more diverse and creates employment outside the largest firms rather than only inside them.
<</list>>
7.1.5. Building AI Literacy and Technical Skills
Section 4.6.3 names AI literacy among developers, policymakers, and the public as one safeguard against authoritarian misuse of AI — a safeguard nobody understands is a safeguard nobody can invoke. What that literacy actually takes to build, at each level from schoolchildren to working engineers, is the subject here.

<<list>>
1. AI in K-12 curricula: introducing AI concepts, and their ethical stakes, before higher education is the only way to reach students who will never take a university AI course at all. AI4K12, a joint initiative of the Association for the Advancement of Artificial Intelligence and the Computer Science Teachers Association launched in 2018, has built national guidelines for K-12 AI education organized around five core ideas: perception, representation and reasoning, learning, natural interaction, and societal impact [[cite:C0388]].

2. Interdisciplinary AI programs at universities: an AI professional trained only in AI is a narrower professional than one trained alongside ethics, social science, and the humanities. MIT's Schwarzman College of Computing runs exactly this kind of program under the name Social and Ethical Responsibilities of Computing, bringing computer scientists together with social scientists and humanists to research and teach the social and ethical implications of computing directly [[cite:C0389]].

3. Specialized training for AI developers: developers building systems meant to act ethically need more than a compliance checklist — training in the ethics, social intelligence, and emotional intelligence that let them recognize a harm their code could cause before a user does.

4. Accessible AI literacy for the general public: courses that reach people well outside the industry are what let a public actually participate in decisions about AI rather than defer to whoever built the system. Elements of AI, a free course built jointly by the University of Helsinki and the Finnish company Reaktor, is the clearest working example — Finland funded its translation into every EU member-state language during its 2019 EU presidency specifically to extend that reach, which makes it a public-private partnership as much as a course [[cite:C0390]].

5. AI education in underrepresented communities: equal access to AI education for communities historically locked out of the field is not a diversity add-on to the rest of this list; a next generation of AI developers drawn from a narrow slice of the population reproduces exactly the narrow set of assumptions section 5.1.1 identifies as a source of AI bias. AI4ALL, a nonprofit that has run AI education programs for underrepresented high-school students on university campuses since 2015, is one organization doing this work directly [[cite:C0391]].
<</list>>
7.1.6. Interdisciplinary Ethics Education and Training
Section 4.4.3 already names cross-training and consultation among developers, ethicists, and legal scholars as one lever for pulling an AI system's immediate environment toward ethical alignment. This section is about the infrastructure that makes that cross-training possible in the first place — where the people who go on to do it actually learn it, across psychology, neuroscience, ethics, law, political economy, and AI itself.

<<h>>Interdisciplinary AI ethics courses at universities<</h>>
Folding an AI ethics course into computer science and engineering degree programs, built around philosophy of AI, social science, and the humanities rather than technical content alone, gives future developers the ethical vocabulary a standalone ethics requirement usually fails to. Harvard's course "Intelligent Systems: Design and Ethical Challenges," designed in 2015 by AI researcher Barbara Grosz together with colleagues from Harvard's philosophy department, works through real case studies — predictive policing, public-health interventions, wildlife conservation — rather than abstract dilemmas, and draws students from the humanities and social sciences as well as computer science [[cite:C0392]].

<<h>>AI ethics and alignment summer schools<</h>>
An intensive, multi-day program that pulls early-career researchers and practitioners out of their home disciplines for a concentrated stretch of training does work a semester-long course cannot: shared problems, cross-disciplinary teams, and the kind of professional network that outlasts the program itself. The Human-Aligned AI Summer School, held annually in Prague and organized jointly by Charles University's Centre for Theoretical Study and a group of independent AI-safety researchers, is a working example, training early-career researchers in AI alignment across an intensive multi-day program [[cite:C0393]].

<<h>>AI ethics hackathons<</h>>
A hackathon format aimed specifically at AI ethics — developers, designers, ethicists, and representatives of an affected community spending a weekend on one concrete social problem — is inexpensive to run and has an obvious institutional home: a computer-science department, an industry AI-ethics team, or a civil-society organization already positioned to supply both the problem statement and the follow-through a single weekend cannot provide on its own.

<<h>>AI ethics certification programs<</h>>
A standardized credential in AI ethics gives developers and other stakeholders a benchmark to work toward and gives employers a way to verify it. The IEEE's CertifAIEd program already does this: a professional certification built around accountability, transparency, privacy, and bias, open to developers and non-developers alike — policymakers, underwriters, human-resources staff — anyone whose work touches an AI system [[cite:C0394]]. Other professional bodies with an existing stake in technology ethics, the Association for Computing Machinery among them, could extend the same model.

<<h>>Industry-academia collaboration<</h>>
Partnerships between industry and academia bridge theoretical ethics research and the practical business of building systems. The MIT-IBM Watson AI Lab, a ten-year, $240 million research partnership launched in 2017, is a working example: among its research pillars is work explicitly aimed at detecting and mitigating bias and building trustworthiness into deployed AI systems [[cite:C0395]].
7.2. Developing Policy Frameworks for AI Ethics, Safety, and Anti-Authoritarianism
Good design inside a single lab does not by itself keep an AI system ethical or resistant to authoritarian misuse. That takes policy: legislation, industry self-regulation, joint public-private funding, and oversight, each covering ground the others cannot reach on their own. National guidelines are the subject of section 7.2.1, self-regulation of 7.2.2, public-private research partnerships of 7.2.3, the personhood question of 7.2.4, and international cooperation of 7.2.5.

Legislation sets the floor that does not depend on a company volunteering for it: obligations on developers and users that can require the same kind of ethics review or independent audit that biomedical research already answers to, applied instead to AI development.

Self-regulation covers what legislation is slow to reach. The Partnership on AI, whose corporate members include Amazon, Apple, and Google, commits its members to "tenets" of safety, transparency, and broad benefit — voluntarily, and without legal force behind the commitment [[cite:C0348]]. What that kind of voluntary commitment is actually worth is the question section 7.2.2 takes up.

Joint funding addresses a narrower problem: research on AI safety and ethics funded solely by the companies whose products it evaluates concentrates the funding and the findings in the same hands. Splitting that funding between government and industry keeps the incentive to find a problem from depending on who is paying to look for it.

None of this holds up without oversight: a body positioned like an institutional review board but built for AI, interdisciplinary and resourced enough to act on what it finds rather than only report it. What such a body specifically has to guard against — mass surveillance, censorship, and the other uses that turn a working system into an instrument of authoritarian control — section 4.6.3 already covers, and section 5.4.3 covers the export-control and prohibited-practices levers that keep a capability from reaching a government that would misuse it. Legislation, self-regulation, funding, and oversight are each partial by themselves; what the rest of this section works out is the combination.
7.2.1. National Policy Guidelines for AI Development, Employment, and Anti-Authoritarianism
National policy for altruistic and anti-authoritarian AI development turns on a handful of recurring areas: interdisciplinary collaboration, innovation policy, and the ethical and safety concerns that run through the rest of this chapter.

<<list>>
1. Value alignment. National policy can fund research on value alignment and on operationalizing ethical principles inside AI systems, rather than leaving that work to whichever lab can afford it. The European Union's High-Level Expert Group on Artificial Intelligence, and the Ethics Guidelines for Trustworthy AI it produced, is one existing template for what a government-convened effort at this looks like [[cite:C0396]].

2. Interdisciplinary collaboration. Funding joint research projects, interdisciplinary centers, and cross-disciplinary training brings AI developers into the same room as ethicists, psychologists, and neuroscientists instead of leaving each discipline to work in isolation. Germany's DFKI, a national AI research institute running since 1988, shows what standing interdisciplinary infrastructure for this can look like, even though its own mandate is broader than ethics alone [[cite:C0397]].

3. Workforce development. AI literacy and technical training are policy investments as much as curricular ones: funding, curriculum development, and partnerships with universities and industry all shape whether a country has anyone qualified to build the systems its own policy calls for. AI Singapore's AI Apprenticeship Programme is one working example, pairing hands-on training with mentorship rather than classroom instruction alone [[cite:C0398]].

4. Public engagement. Citizen assemblies, public consultations, and NGO partnerships give AI policy something a corporate ethics board cannot supply on its own: input from people who are not experts in the technology but are its subjects. The UK's Centre for Data Ethics and Innovation, a government advisory body established in 2018, is a working example of standing infrastructure for this rather than a one-off consultation [[cite:C0399]].

5. Safety research and risk mitigation. Targeted funding for robustness, generalizability, and long-term safety research, paired with safety guidelines developed jointly with international partners, keeps safety work from depending entirely on which lab happens to prioritize it. The Partnership on AI — a coalition of companies, researchers, and civil-society organizations, not a government body — is one existing venue where that kind of information-sharing already happens [[cite:C0348]].

6. Legal and regulatory frameworks. Data privacy, cybersecurity, accountability, and — where a system's capacities warrant it — legal status are all areas where existing law needs updating rather than displacing wholesale. Section 5.4.3 works through what a jurisdiction's regulatory toolkit for AI looks like in practice, including the EU's AI Act; section 7.2.4 and section 7.4 take up the legal-status question specifically. What belongs here is the harmonization problem those sections leave open: a regulation with real teeth in one country does little if a developer can simply relocate to one without it, which is why a national framework eventually has to answer to an international one too.

7. Encouraging self-regulation. Section 7.2.2 takes up what industry self-regulation can and cannot do on its own, including the doublespeak risk this item's own title warns against. What national policy adds is the leverage self-regulation lacks by design: voluntary standards and certification programs backed by government monitoring carry a cost for noncompliance that a purely voluntary code does not.
<</list>>
7.2.2. Encouraging Industry Best Practices and Self-regulation
Self-regulation is industries developing and enforcing their own standards without government intervention — agile and responsive to technological change in a way legislation rarely is, and just as easily a cover for avoiding the accountability legislation would impose. Both things are true at once, and the rest of this section works out which one dominates in practice.

<<h>>The Dangers of Corporate Doublespeak in AI Ethics<</h>>
Corporate doublespeak — deliberately ambiguous, evasive, or inflated language meant to project ethical seriousness a company's actual practice does not support — is how self-regulation fails without anyone having to admit it failed. A commitment costs a company nothing if nothing enforces it.

The gap between statement and practice shows up first as erosion: a company announces a value and does not act as though it holds it afterward. Google's AI Principles, published in June 2018 and pledging to avoid AI applications built for weapons or mass surveillance, were themselves a response to months of internal and public backlash over Project Maven, a Pentagon contract that used Google's machine learning to analyze drone footage — thousands of employees signed a letter opposing the company's involvement, and roughly a dozen resigned [[cite:C0400]]. Having a set of principles is not the same as having upheld them since; the real test of the pledge is what happened after it was made, not that it was made. OpenAI's history makes a related point from a different angle: founded on a public commitment to open publication, it withheld the full version of GPT-2 in 2019 over misuse concerns — a safety-motivated retreat from its own transparency norm that was itself controversial — and in the same year restructured from a nonprofit into a capped-profit company to raise the capital its research increasingly required [[cite:C0401]]. Neither move was dishonest on its face. Both show how a stated commitment to transparency erodes the moment an organization has a competing incentive strong enough to test it.

Public trust erodes the same way, faster and more visibly. In 2020, Google forced out Timnit Gebru, a co-lead of its Ethical AI team, after a dispute over a paper examining risks in large language models; Margaret Mitchell, the team's other co-lead, was dismissed the following year after searching for evidence of how Gebru had been treated [[cite:C0402]]. Thousands of Google employees and outside researchers signed a letter in response, and the episode became a reference point for a specific kind of skepticism: a company can fund and publicize an ethics research team while still overriding it the moment its findings become inconvenient. Whether the internal changes Google made afterward addressed that problem or only the public exposure it created is a judgment the affected researchers have made publicly themselves, and it is not one this book needs to make on their behalf.

External oversight is what would catch doublespeak that internal incentives don't stop. Facebook's content-moderation Oversight Board is one attempt at building it: a body structurally separated from Facebook's day-to-day decisions and funded through an irrevocable trust that Meta alone capitalized — a design meant to answer exactly the criticism that self-funded oversight is not real oversight, though critics have noted that a trust a company creates and funds once is not the same thing as a board with no relationship to the company it reviews [[cite:C0403]]. Whether that distinction holds up is a fair question to ask of any self-regulatory body a company funds itself, not a specific indictment of this one.

Misrepresentation of what a system can actually do rounds out the pattern. Clearview AI marketed its facial-recognition technology with claims of near-100-percent accuracy [[cite:C0404]], a figure independent testing did not support, and regulators in France and Italy later fined the company on separate grounds — unlawful collection of the biometric data the system was trained on [[cite:C0377]]. A company that overstates what its own system can do is doing a milder version of what doublespeak does with ethics commitments: describing the product it wishes it had built rather than the one that exists.

None of this is fixable by asking companies to doublespeak less. The same incentive that produces vague ethics language also has an obvious interest in the regulation that would eventually replace it — a version of the same behavior that is harder to document than a corporate pledge, because it happens before any public commitment exists to measure it against. What actually stops doublespeak is external enforcement: regulation with real penalties, audits a company cannot decline, and oversight bodies whose funding and staffing do not run back to the company they review.

<<h>>Consequences of AI Self-regulation and Potential Power Imbalances<</h>>
Self-regulation concentrates power by default, not by conspiracy: whoever writes the standard also decides what counts as compliant, and a company self-regulating its own product is answering both questions itself. Amazon's Rekognition facial-recognition software is a documented instance of what that produces in practice — an MIT Media Lab audit found significantly higher error rates for darker-skinned and female faces than for the lighter-skinned male faces the system had apparently been tuned and tested against most closely [[cite:C0405]]. The bias was not caught by Amazon's own self-regulation; it took an outside audit the company did not commission.

The opacity problem this section's first head already covers with Google's Project Maven applies here too, from a different angle: a company operating without mandatory external review can prioritize competitiveness over the societal impact of what it ships, and nothing in a self-regulatory framework forces that trade-off into the open before it has already been made. The conflict-of-interest problem the Facebook Oversight Board illustrates above is the same structural issue applied to review bodies specifically: a company-funded board faces a version of the independence question any self-funded oversight has to answer, however genuinely it is designed to answer it.

What self-regulation cannot supply on its own is uniformity. Without an enforceable baseline, standards vary by company; a smaller developer without the resources for a well-staffed internal ethics function competes at a disadvantage against one that can afford the appearance of one, and a consumer has no reliable way to tell which AI system's ethical claims mean anything. Section 5.4.3 covers what a binding baseline looks like in practice, including the EU AI Act's requirements for high-risk systems — the kind of enforceable floor self-regulation, by definition, cannot set for itself.

None of this argues that self-regulation is worthless, only that it needs something outside itself to answer to. A synergy between government-set baselines and industry-led standards — mandatory requirements at the floor, voluntary practice above it — gets more of the agility self-regulation offers without giving up the accountability it structurally cannot provide alone.

<<h>>Assessing the Effectiveness of AI Self-regulation<</h>>
Whether self-regulation is working is itself measurable: the transparency of a company's stated ethical policies, how quickly it responds when a problem surfaces, and whether its safety commitments hold up under outside scrutiny are things a reader can check rather than take on faith.

The Partnership on AI, the industry coalition already covered in section 7.2.1, is a useful test case precisely because its own design limits what it can enforce: member companies interpret its guidelines individually, and nothing in the partnership's structure obliges any of them to follow through [[cite:C0348]]. OpenAI's 2018 Charter is a narrower, single-company version of the same instrument, committing the organization to prioritizing safety research and to collaborating with other research and policy institutions rather than racing them [[cite:C0406]] — and the same question applies to a charter as to a coalition's tenets: a document is only as good as what happens when honoring it costs something. The Gebru and Mitchell dismissals this section's first head already covers are the clearest test of that question this chapter has: public scrutiny, not the company's own stated commitments, was what actually produced a response.

Third-party audits, external advisory boards with real authority, and cross-stakeholder collaboration between regulators, researchers, and social scientists are what turn a stated commitment to ethics into something checkable rather than a claim taken on faith. A handful of concrete indicators do the checking:

<<list>>
- Clear, actionable ethical guidelines that are actually adopted and enforced, not just published.
- Transparent disclosure of ethical policies, stated objectives, and progress against them.
- A documented track record of responding to ethical incidents promptly, with real accountability attached.
- Internal enforcement mechanisms, including employee training that goes beyond a compliance formality.
- Ongoing internal evaluation of AI-related practices, paired with active research into emerging ethical questions.
- Substantive participation in multi-stakeholder collaborations like the Partnership on AI, rather than nominal membership.
<</list>>

A company that clears most of these is doing more than most competitors. A company that clears none of them while describing itself as an ethics leader is the doublespeak this section opened with, restated in a different register.
7.2.3. Fostering Public-Private Partnerships for AI Research and Innovation
Public-private partnerships pool government and private-sector resources, knowledge, and expertise in ways neither side manages alone. Two existing examples show what that looks like applied to AI research specifically — and what adapting either one toward altruistic, anti-authoritarian goals would actually require.

<<list>>
1. The National Science Foundation's National AI Research Institutes, launched in 2020 and expanded to more than twenty institutes the following year, pair federal funding with industry co-funders — Amazon, Google, and Intel among them — and address themes from AI safety to human-AI interaction to climate applications [[cite:C0407]]. A program structured the same way but aimed specifically at altruistic and anti-authoritarian AI research, rather than folding that goal into a broader institute's mandate, is a model worth adapting directly.

2. The International Telecommunication Union's AI for Good initiative, a United Nations agency program launched in 2017, brings together governments, academic institutions, and companies — IBM, Microsoft, and NVIDIA have all participated through it — around AI applications for poverty, health, education, and climate [[cite:C0408]]. Extending that kind of platform to carry an explicit brief on democratic values and resistance to authoritarian misuse, rather than assuming general "ethical AI" language already covers it, is the adaptation altruistic and anti-authoritarian AI goals would need.
<</list>>

Neither partnership was designed around anti-authoritarian AI specifically, and that gap is the point: a public-private partnership is only as good as the mandate written into it, and the self-regulation risk section 7.2.2 examines — a partner treating participation as public relations rather than practice — applies here too.
7.2.4. Legal and ethical implications of AI systems gaining personhood
Whether an AI system should ever hold personhood is worked out in depth elsewhere in this chapter: section 7.4.1 explains why the underlying science of sentience offers no clean threshold for law to seize on, and how a jurisdiction can set an operational one anyway; section 7.4.2 works through what personhood would actually mean case by case — who answers for a diagnostic system's fatal error, what happens to a companion system when its owner dies — and shows this is not a hypothetical debate, citing a real legislative attempt at exactly this move and the researchers who explained why it was premature. That is the substance, and this section does not re-argue it.

What belongs here, in the policy-frameworks part of the book, is narrower: who decides, and when. Every version of this question that has actually reached a legislature so far has arrived as a response to pressure — a liability dispute, a public controversy — rather than from a standing body asking it in advance. Section 7.4.1's interdisciplinary review body only does the work it is designed for if some jurisdiction creates it before a forcing case shows up. That is a policy choice, not a doctrinal one: a national AI strategy that names an agency, gives it a mandate, and sets a trigger for revisiting the question — one of the options section 7.4.2 already lays out — decides this in advance rather than under the pressure of whichever incident forces the issue first.
7.2.5. Encouraging the Development of Altruistic, Anti-Authoritarian, and Ethical AI Systems
Sections 7.2.1 through 7.2.4 covered the instruments — national guidelines, self-regulation, public-private partnerships, legal status. What they are instruments for is the subject of this closing section: AI development that is altruistic and anti-authoritarian by design, not as an afterthought bolted onto a system built without either goal in mind.

<<h>>Policies to Counteract Authoritarian Uses of AI<</h>>
Section 5.4.3 already covers the core policy toolkit for this — export controls, prohibited-practices regulation, and developer-facing standards — and section 4.6.3 covers the safeguards a system needs built in from the start. Two pieces of that toolkit are worth adding here: measurement, and a standing international body.

A government cannot restrict what it cannot see. The Carnegie Endowment's AI Global Surveillance (AIGS) Index, published in 2019, catalogued AI-enabled surveillance technology in use across a large majority of the world's countries, documenting how far the practice had already spread rather than treating it as a hypothetical risk [[cite:C0409]]. An index like this is not itself a countermeasure — it is the evidence base that makes the case for one, and for whichever export controls or prohibited-practices rules a jurisdiction decides its evidence justifies.

The Global Partnership on Artificial Intelligence, initiated by G7 countries in 2018 and launched in 2020, does for state-level AI policy roughly what the Partnership on AI does for industry: a standing forum rather than a one-time agreement [[cite:C0410]]. The difference is membership — GPAI's members are national governments, which is the piece section 7.2.1 already noted the Partnership on AI itself does not have.

<<h>>AI Systems That Support Democratic Values<</h>>
Section 4.4.6 already works through what this looks like with real, working examples: Google's Perspective API for scoring content toxicity without necessarily removing it, and Taiwan's vTaiwan process for gathering structured public input on contested policy questions — along with the caution that belongs next to both. A system built to flag disinformation runs on the same underlying capability as a system built to identify dissidents; the difference is governance, not engineering.

One gap those examples leave open: election-integrity monitoring specifically, an AI system built to flag anomalies in real-time reporting or detect coordinated interference during a vote. What would make a system like that trustworthy is the same list section 4.4.6 already gives for any AI role in democratic process — privacy-preserving design, an audit trail, and a multi-stakeholder review body that includes more than whichever party happens to hold power at the time — applied to the specific case of an election rather than restated as a fresh list here.

<<h>>International Cooperation on Shared Standards<</h>>
The case for international cooperation on shared AI ethics standards — joint research, common guidelines, treaties, capacity-building — is made at length elsewhere in this book: section 5.4.3 makes it for the anti-authoritarian case specifically and hands the general question of coordination between states to section 6.3, which is where it belongs. Restating that argument's shape here would not strengthen it.

What is worth adding is one concrete instance of it happening.
